Kalai’s flag conjecture for locally anti-blocking polytopes
Arnon Chor

Abstract
We prove Kalai’s full flag conjecture for the class of locally anti-blocking polytopes, and show that there is equality if and only if the polytope is a (generalized) Hanner polytope.



1
Introduction and main theorem

For polytopes, central symmetry plays a central role in the combinatorial structure. For example, the Figiel-Lindenstrauss-Milman inequality
[
5
]
tells us that a centrally symmetric polytope cannot have few facets and also few vertices. But what about faces of other dimensions? The question of counting all faces of centrally symmetric polytopes is the subject of a famous conjecture by Kalai
[
6
]
.

Conjecture 1.1

(
3^{d}
conjecture)
.

Any centrally symmetric
d
-dimensional polytope has at least
3^{d}
non-empty faces, with equality if and only if the polytope is a linear image of a Hanner polytope.

Hanner polytopes, the conjectured equality cases, are recursively defined to be either the symmetric segment
[-1,1]
in
\mathbb{R}^{1}
, the polar dual
P^{\circ}=\left\{x\ \middle|\ \forall y\in P:\left\langle x,y\right\rangle\leq 1\right\}
of some previously defined Hanner polytope
P
, or the convex hull
P\vee Q
of two previously defined lower-dimensional Hanner polytopes embedded in some two orthogonal subspaces. Hanner polytopes are also the conjectured minimizers for the famous Mahler conjecture
[
7
]
:

Conjecture 1.2

(Mahler’s conjecture)
.

For any centrally symmetric
d
-dimensional convex body
K
,

\vol(K)\cdot\vol(K^{\circ})\geq\frac{4^{d}}{d!},

with equality if and only if
K
is a linear image of a Hanner polytope.

A (full) flag of a polytope
P
is a chain of faces of
P
, one in each dimension. A later conjecture, also attributed to Kalai in
[
12
]
has to do with flags of centrally symmetric polytopes.

Conjecture 1.3

(Kalai’s (full) flag conjecture)
.

Any centrally symmetric
d
-dimensional polytope has at least
2^{d}\cdot d!
flags, with equality if and only if the polytope is a linear image of a Hanner polytope.

Recall that a polytope
P
is called
locally anti-blocking
if for any
x\in P
and coordinate subspace
H
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\proj_{H}P=P\cap H
for
\proj_{H}
the orthogonal projection onto
H
. Note that a locally anti-blocking polytope need not be centrally symmetric. Locally anti-blocking polytopes and 1-unconditional polytopes (i.e. polytopes symmetric w.r.t. reflection about any coordinate hyperplane) are a natural subclass for testing conjectures: for example, Mahler’s conjecture
[
1
,
10
]
and Godbersen’s conjecture
[
1
,
9
]
have been verified for this subclass, and the Hadwiger-Boltyanski illumination conjecture has been settled in the related class of 1-symmetric polytopes (
[
15
]
, see also
[
13
,
14
]
).
Recently
[
11
,
2
]
, the
3^{d}
conjecture was also proven for locally anti-blocking polytopes, and the flag conjecture was proven
[
4
]
for 1-unconditional polytopes (albeit without the equality case). The proof in
[
4
]
uses highly non-elementary tools from Funk geometry. The main theorem of this paper is a strengthening of this result, and equally importantly, has an elementary inductive proof. Recall that generalized Hanner polytopes are defined similarly to Hanner polytopes, with the addition that in
\mathbb{R}^{1}
any segment with
0
in its interior is also defined to be generalized Hanner. A locally anti-blocking polytope is called
proper
if the origin is in its interior.

Theorem 1.4
.

Any
d
-dimensional proper locally anti-blocking polytope has at least
2^{d}\cdot d!
flags, with equality if and only if the polytope is a generalized Hanner polytope.

A locally anti-blocking polytope
P
is called
normalized
if for any
e_{i}
a standard basis vector of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the support function
h_{P}(v)=\max_{x\in P}\left\langle x,v\right\rangle
satisfies
h_{P}(\pm e_{i})=1
(see
[
11
]
). A normalized locally anti-blocking polytope is always contained in
\square^{d}
, the unit cube
[-1,1]^{d}
, and always contains
\lozenge^{d}
, its polar dual. Therefore, any normalized locally anti-blocking polytope is automatically proper. Note that a normalized generalized Hanner polytope is just a Hanner polytope.

Given a locally anti-blocking polytope
P
, consider the map
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by

x\mapsto\sum_{i\in[d]:x_{i}\neq 0}\frac{x_{i}}{h_{P}(\sign x_{i}\cdot e_{i})}e_{i}.

The image of
P
under this map is always a normalized locally anti-blocking polytope, and also has the same combinatorial structure as
P
itself (see the notion of
halfspace scaling
in
[
11
]
). Thus,
Theorem

1.4
is equivalent to the following statement, which is the version we proceed to prove.

Theorem 1.5
.

Any
d
-dimensional normalized locally anti-blocking polytope has at least
2^{d}\cdot d!
flags, with equality if and only if the polytope is a Hanner polytope.

2
Notations and preliminaries

We denote by
B
the unit Euclidean ball (the dimension will be clear from context),
[d]=\{1,...,d\}
, and
[k,d]=\{k,...,d\}
for
k\leq d
integers. We denote the convex hull of a set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\bigvee X
. The relative interior
\relint X
of some set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined as the interior of
X
in the topology induced by
\aff X
, the affine span of
X
.

For a closed convex set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
K(\nu)=\left\{x\in K\ \middle|\ \left\langle x,\nu\right\rangle=\sup_{K}\left\langle\cdot,\nu\right\rangle\right\}
. A set of this form is called a face of
K
, and by convention the empty set
\emptyset
is also a face of
K
. Denote by
\mathcal{F}(K)
the set of faces of
K
, including the empty face (which is of dimension -1 by convention) and
K
itself. The faces
\emptyset
and
K
are called
non-proper faces
. Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{i}(K)
the set of faces of
K
of dimension
i
.
A polyhedron is a closed convex set with a finite number of faces; equivalently, it is the intersection of a finite number of half-spaces. A polytope is a compact polyhedron. We call faces of a polyhedron of codimension 1
facets
, and faces of dimension 1
edges
. For a point
x\in P
, the support of
x
in
P
,
\supp_{P}x
, is the unique face
F\in\mathcal{F}(P)
with
x\in\relint F
, or equivalently, the unique minimal face of
P
that contains
x
. Similarly, for a set
A\subseteq P
we denote by
\supp_{P}A
the minimal face of
P
that contains
A
. For a non-empty face
F\in\mathcal{F}(P)
, denote by
N_{P}F=\left\{\nu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \middle|\ P(\nu)\supseteq F\right\}
the
normal cone
of
F
in
P
.

Recall that a (polyhedral) cone is a set which is positively spanned by a finite number of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. A
fan
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a finite family
\Phi
of polyhedral cones such that
\bigcup_{C\in\Phi}C=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and for any
C,D\in\Phi
,
C\cap D
is a face of both
C
and
D
, and is also a member of
\Phi
. We denote the set of
k
-dimensional cones of a fan
\Phi
by
\Phi_{k}
.
For any fan
\Phi
and point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote by
\supp_{\Phi}p\in\Phi
the minimal (w.r.t. inclusion) cone in
\Phi
that contains
p
. For
J\subseteq[d]
denote by
\mathbb{R}^{J}
the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
linearly spanned by the standard Euclidean basis vectors
\{e_{j}\}_{j\in J}
; a subspace of this form is called a
coordinate subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

A flag of a polyhedron
P
of dimension
d
is a collection
F=(F_{-1},F_{0},F_{1},...,F_{d})
with
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{i}(P)
for all
i\in[-1,d]
and
F_{i}\subsetneq F_{j}
for
i<j
. The set of flags of
P
will be denoted
\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1116.82pt{\vbox to1432.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-217.60083pt\lower-1615.20616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}(P)
. We note that this notion is sometimes referred to in the literature as a
full
or
complete
flag.

2.1
Sketch of the proof and plan of the paper

Let
P
be a proper locally anti-blocking polytope and consider the standard fan
{\Phi_{st}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which consists of cones positively spanned by all subsets of
\{\pm e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
which don’t contain both
\pm e_{i}
for any
i
(that is,
{\Phi_{st}}
is the fan containing all the orthants in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
). We start by introducing the notion of a
sign
of a flag
F\in\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1116.82pt{\vbox to1432.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-217.60083pt\lower-1615.20616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

in
Section

2.2
. The sign of
F
, denoted
\sign F
, is defined as the minimal cone in
{\Phi_{st}}
that intersects all the relative interiors of the faces composing
F
, and it is shown that
\dim\sign F\geq\dim P
. Therefore for the full-dimensionsional polytope
P
, the signs of all its flags are full dimensional cones in
{\Phi_{st}}
.

, we denote by

\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}^{C}}(P)
the set of flags of the intersection
P\cap\lin C
that have sign exactly
C
, where
\lin C=\aff C-\aff C
is the linear span. Note that these are not flags of
P
itself, but of a section, see
Figure

1
for an example.

We count the flags of
P
inductively as follows. For any cone
D\in{\Phi_{st}}
and one of its facets
C
(which is also a cone of
{\Phi_{st}}
of course), we build an injection
\chi_{C}^{D}:{\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1116.82pt{\vbox to1432.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-217.60083pt\lower-1615.20616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

This is carried out in
Section

3
.

For the equality case, we follow the strategy of Sanyal and Winter in
[
11
]
: assume
P
is a normalized locally anti-blocking polytope in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and assume it has the correct number of flags. First, in
Section

4
, we show that for any coordinate hyperplane
H
,
P\cap H
also has the correct number of flags. This allows us to define the graph
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
, defined in
Section

2.4
, which encodes the combinatorial structure of
P
itself: in fact,
P
can be recovered from
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
in this case, see
Section

4
. We use the classification in
Section

4
to assert that since all coordinate sections of
P
are also minimizing,
P
must be a Hanner polytope. This is done in
Section

4
.

Let us give some more details.

x
y
z

x
y
z

Figure 1:
Two examples of some flag
F
(in green) and
\chi_{C}^{D}F
(in blue), where
C
is the cone spanned by
x,y
and
D
is spanned by
x,y,z
.

2.2
Flags of polytopes and their signs

\Phi
. We note that in the rest of the paper, we will only use the standard fan
{\Phi_{st}}

\par}(P)
, we first show that there is a cone in
\Phi
that intersects the relative interiors of all the faces
F_{i}
, and then show that the set of cones that have this property is closed under intersections. The intersection of all these cones will be denoted
\sign_{\Phi}F
, the
sign
of
F
.

We start with a simple fact about simplices.

Fact 2.1
.

Let
S
be a
k
-dimensional simplex in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be points such that
H=\aff(S\vee\{x,y\})
is
(k+1)
-dimensional, and that
x,y
lie in the same connected component of
H\setminus\aff S
. Then
\relint\left(x\vee S\right)\cap\relint\left(y\vee S\right)\neq\emptyset
.

.

. Since

is a flag of

(k-1)
-dimensional, by induction there is a nested sequence of cones

as above. Pick some

.

As
x\in\relint C_{k-1}
, there is some

such that

x+rB\subseteq\bigcup_{C_{k-1}\subseteq D\in\Phi}D.

(1)

Since
x\in F_{k-1}
is in the relative boundary of
F_{k}
, there is some

.
Consider
C_{k}=\supp_{\Phi}y
. By (
1
),
C_{k}\supseteq C_{k-1}
, and by construction,
y\in\relint C\cap\relint F_{k}
.

Additionally, let
C\in\Phi
be a cone that has
C\cap\relint F_{i}\neq\emptyset
for all
i\in[0,k]
. Choose points

. As the points
x_{i}
lie in the relative interiors of their corresponding faces

, they must be in general position, and so do not all belong to any
(k-1)
-dimensional affine subspace. Since they all belong to
C
, it must hold that
\dim C\geq k
.
∎

\par}(P)
. The set of cones
C\in\Phi
for which
C\cap\relint F_{i}\neq\emptyset
for all
i\in[0,k]
is closed under intersection. That is, if the above condition holds for
C,D\in\Phi
, then it also holds for
C\cap D
.

Proof.

Let
C,D\in\Phi
and assume that for any
i\in[0,k]
,
\relint F_{i}\cap C
and
\relint F_{i}\cap D
are nonempty. We will show that for any
i\in[0,k]
,
\relint F_{i}\cap C\cap D\neq\emptyset
by induction on
i
.

For
i=0
, there is a unique point
p\in\relint F_{0}
and by hypothesis
p\in C\cap D
.

Assume the statement is true for any
j\in[0,i-1]
, we will show it holds for
i
as well. By the induction hypothesis, there are points
x_{j}\in\relint F_{j}\cap C\cap D
, for
j\in[0,i-1]
.
Choose some
x\in\relint F_{i}\cap C
and
y\in\relint F_{i}\cap D
.
We first note that
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{i-1}\cup\{x,y\}
all belong to an
i
-dimensional affine subspace, namely
\aff F_{i}
, and that
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{i-1}
affinely span
\aff F_{i-1}
. Next, note that
x
and
y
lie on the same connected component of
\aff F_{i}\setminus\aff F_{i-1}
, since
F_{i-1}
is a facet of
F_{i}
.
By
Section

2.2
, there is some
z\in\relint\left(x\vee\bigvee_{j=0}^{i-1}x_{j}\right)\cap\relint\left(y\vee\bigvee_{j=0}^{i-1}x_{j}\right)\subseteq C\cap D
. Since
x\vee\bigvee_{j=0}^{i-1}x_{j}
and
y\vee\bigvee_{j=0}^{i-1}x_{j}
both affinely span
F_{i}
and are contained in it, we see that
z\in\relint F_{i}
. See
Figure

2
.
∎

x_{0}
x_{1}
x
y
C
D

Figure 2:
The cones
C,D
intersecting at a plane; a point
x\in\relint F_{2}\cap C
(in red); a point
y\in\relint F_{2}\cap D
(in blue); and points
(x_{j})_{j=0}^{1}
(in purple).

We are ready to define our flag signs.

.

it is closed under intersections. Denote by

the intersection of this set. This cone will be called the sign of the flag

. It follows from

and
2.2
that

.

,

, and two faces

, there are exactly two
i
-dimensional faces
H\in\mathcal{F}_{i}(P)
such that
F_{i-1}\subseteq H\subseteq F_{i+1}
. This property is called the
diamond property
of polytopes, and will be widely used in this work.

Note that

. Therefore, since

, we have

, in contradiction to the choice of
k_{0}

Saying that the projection of a flag, in a certain direction
n
, results in a flag, is actually a statement about the interaction of the direction
n
and the normal cones to the faces in the flag, which form a flag themselves (i.e. they are nested). Thus
Section

2.3
is proven by first showing the dual statement:

\par}(P)
. There exists a unique facet
G\in\mathcal{F}_{d-1}(P)
that contains
F_{0}
and such that
(F_{i}\cap G)_{i\in[d]}
is a flag of
G
. Furthermore,

G=(r_{d-1}r_{d-2}...r_{1}F)_{d-1}.

Proof.

G=(r_{d-1}r_{d-2}...r_{1}F)_{d-1}

\par}(G)
. For any
i\in[d-1]
denote
F^{i}=r_{i}...r_{2}r_{1}F\in\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1116.82pt{\vbox to1432.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-217.60083pt\lower-1615.20616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1845.31 -1741.25 C 1717.64 -1841.62 1568.94 -1891.8 1399.2 -1891.8 C 1318.03 -1891.8 1235.37 -1880.36 1151.24 -1857.49 C 1031.69 -1825.75 971.91 -1780 971.91 -1720.22 L 971.91 -1352.71 C 935.02 -1348.28 908.45 -1346.07 892.21 -1346.07 C 722.48 -1346.07 571.19 -1409.9 438.36 -1537.57 L 438.36 -2233.85 L 329.88 -2233.85 L 329.88 -386.33 C 311.43 -370.1 302.2 -350.91 302.2 -328.77 C 302.2 -278.59 329.14 -253.49 383.01 -253.49 C 438.36 -253.49 466.03 -278.59 466.03 -328.77 C 466.03 -348.69 456.81 -367.88 438.36 -386.33 L 438.36 -544.63 C 578.57 -423.6 728.75 -363.08 888.89 -363.08 C 1116.93 -363.08 1230.94 -461.24 1230.94 -657.54 L 1230.94 -896.64 C 1284.82 -905.5 1337.58 -909.92 1389.24 -909.92 C 1556.76 -909.92 1708.78 -860.85 1845.31 -762.7 L 1845.31 -1741.25 Z M 1791.07 -1716.9 L 1791.07 -867.86 C 1656.76 -932.06 1525.03 -964.17 1395.88 -964.17 C 1333.89 -964.17 1278.91 -959.74 1230.94 -950.88 L 1230.94 -1463.41 C 1230.94 -1523.18 1199.21 -1567.09 1135.75 -1595.14 L 1105.86 -1608.42 C 1043.13 -1636.46 1011.77 -1665.61 1011.77 -1695.87 C 1011.77 -1743.84 1068.22 -1781.47 1181.13 -1808.78 C 1261.57 -1827.97 1336.47 -1837.56 1405.84 -1837.56 C 1547.54 -1837.56 1675.94 -1797.34 1791.07 -1716.9 Z M 1176.7 -1349.39 L 1176.7 -665.29 C 1176.7 -499.98 1080.77 -417.33 888.89 -417.33 C 735.39 -417.33 585.22 -483.37 438.36 -615.47 L 438.36 -1460.09 C 572.67 -1347.18 725.06 -1290.72 895.53 -1290.72 C 995.9 -1290.72 1089.62 -1310.28 1176.7 -1349.39 Z M 1189.99 -1453.44 C 1189.99 -1406.21 1135.38 -1375.22 1026.16 -1360.46 L 1026.16 -1587.39 C 1135.38 -1534.25 1189.99 -1489.6 1189.99 -1453.44 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}(P)
, and also denote
F^{0}=F
. In this notation,
G=(F^{d-1})_{d-1}
. Note that we have
(F^{i})_{j}=(F^{k})_{j}
for any
i,j,k\in[0,d-1]
such that
i,k<j
or
i,k\geq j
, since
r_{l}
only changes the face in dimension
l
, for any
l
.
First let us show that for any
i\in[0,d-2]
,

(F^{i})_{i}=F_{i+1}\cap G.

Indeed, by the above,
(F^{i})_{i}=(F^{d-1})_{i}\subseteq(F^{d-1})_{d-1}=G
, and also
(F^{i})_{i}\subseteq(F^{i})_{i+1}=(F^{0})_{i+1}=F_{i+1}
, so
(F^{i})_{i}\subseteq F_{i+1}\cap G
. But
F_{i+1}\not\subseteq G
by
Section

2.3
, which implies that
\dim F_{i+1}\cap G\leq i
, and of course
\dim F^{i}_{i}=i
. Therefore they are equal.

Specifically, using
i=0
, we get
F_{0}=(F^{0})_{0}=F_{1}\cap G
and so
F_{0}\subseteq G
. Considering
0\leq i\leq d-2
, we get that
F_{i+1}\cap G=(F^{i})_{i}=(F^{d-1})_{i}
, and since
F_{d}\cap G=P\cap G=(F^{d-1})_{d-1}
, we see that
(F_{i}\cap G)_{i\in[d]}
is just
((F^{d-1})_{i})_{i=0}^{d-1}
, which is a flag of
G
by construction.

Next, let
G^{\prime}
be a facet of
P
such that
F_{0}\subseteq G^{\prime}
and assume that
(F_{i}\cap G^{\prime})_{i\in[d]}
is a flag of
G^{\prime}
. We will show that for any
i\in[0,d]
,
F_{i}\cap G^{\prime}=F_{i}\cap G
by induction on
i
.

Indeed, for
i=0
the condition is guaranteed since
F_{0}\subseteq G^{\prime}
by hypothesis. Assume that
F_{i}\cap G^{\prime}=F_{i}\cap G
, we will show that
F_{i+1}\cap G^{\prime}=F_{i+1}\cap G
. The face
F_{i}\cap G^{\prime}
is of dimension
i-1
due to its location in the sequence of faces
(F_{j}\cap G^{\prime})_{j\in[d]}
, which is a sequence of faces of dimensions
0,1,2,...,d-1
. Therefore
F_{i}\not\subseteq G^{\prime}
, since if
F_{i}\subseteq G^{\prime}
then
\dim F_{i}\cap G^{\prime}=\dim F_{i}=i
, a contradiction. By the same argument,
F_{i}\not\subseteq G
. Additionally, both
F_{i+1}\cap G^{\prime}
and
F_{i+1}\cap G
are faces of
P
contained in
F_{i+1}
and containing
F_{i}\cap G^{\prime}=F_{i}\cap G
. By the diamond property there are exactly two faces of
P
between
F_{i}\cap G^{\prime}
and
F_{i+1}
. Note that
F_{i}
is one of these faces, and that neither
F_{i+1}\cap G^{\prime}
nor
F_{i+1}\cap G
can equal
F_{i}
since that would imply
F_{i}\subseteq G^{\prime}
or
F_{i}\subseteq G
. Thus
F_{i+1}\cap G^{\prime}=F_{i+1}\cap G
.

Using
i=d
we get
G^{\prime}=F_{d}\cap G^{\prime}=F_{d}\cap G=G
.
∎

Proof of
Section

2.3
.

This proof is a standard duality argument: for any polytope
P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
k\in[0,d]
there is a bijection

m_{P}:\mathcal{F}_{k}(P)_{\geq F_{0}}\to\mathcal{F}_{d-k}(N_{P}F_{0})

given by
L\mapsto N_{P}L
, where
\mathcal{F}_{k}(P)_{\geq F_{0}}
denotes the set of faces of
P
containing
F_{0}
of dimension
k
. The inverse map given by
G\mapsto(F_{0}+G^{\perp})\cap P
. Further, for any
E\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(P)_{\geq F_{0}}
and
L\in\mathcal{F}_{k}(P)_{\geq F_{0}}
, denoting
L^{\prime}=\proj_{E^{\perp}}L
and
P^{\prime}=\proj_{E^{\perp}}P
,

m_{P^{\prime}}(\supp_{P^{\prime}}L^{\prime})\cap E^{\perp}=m_{P}(L)\cap E^{\perp},

(2)

or in other words,
N_{P^{\prime}}(\supp_{P^{\prime}}L^{\prime})\cap E^{\perp}=N_{P}L\cap E^{\perp}
.
Indeed, for any
\nu\in E^{\perp}
we have

\nu\in N_{P}L\Leftrightarrow L\subseteq P(\nu)\Leftrightarrow L^{\prime}\subseteq P^{\prime}(\nu)\Leftrightarrow\supp_{P^{\prime}}L^{\prime}\subseteq P^{\prime}(\nu)\Leftrightarrow\nu\in N_{P^{\prime}}(\supp_{P^{\prime}}L^{\prime}).

Since
m_{P^{\prime}}
is order-reversing, we see that
(\supp_{P^{\prime}}\proj_{E^{\perp}}F_{i})_{i=0}^{d-1}
is a flag of
P^{\prime}
if and only if
(m_{P^{\prime}}(\supp_{P^{\prime}}\proj_{E^{\perp}}F_{d-i-1})\cap E^{\perp})_{i=0}^{d-1}
is a flag of
N_{P}F_{0}\cap E^{\perp}
. Using this fact, (
2
), and then
Section

2.3
with the flag
(m_{P}(F_{d-k}))_{k=0}^{d}
, we get existence and uniqueness of the edge
E
. Since this duality is order-reversing, we have
r_{k}\circ m=m\circ r_{d-k}
, where here
m
is considered as a map
\left\{G\in\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1116.82pt{\vbox to1432.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-217.60083pt\lower-1615.20616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

This proves the ’furthermore’ statement.
∎

2.4
Some properties of locally anti-blocking polytopes

In this subsection we recall several useful propositions, and the definition of the graph
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
which encodes useful combinatorial data about the polytope
P
. We start with the following proposition, rephrased in the language of the standard fan
{\Phi_{st}}
.

Proposition 2.8

(
[
11
, Proposition 8]
)
.

Let
P
be a locally anti-blocking polytope in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
F\in\mathcal{F}(P)
, and
C,D\in{\Phi_{st}}
. If
\relint F\cap\relint C\neq\emptyset
and
\relint F\cap\relint D\neq\emptyset
then
N_{P}F\subseteq\lin(C\cap D)
.

We next recall the definition of
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
from
[
11
]
, see also
[
8
]
for the 1-unconditional case.
Let
P
be a proper locally anti-blocking polytope. Assume that
P
has the following property: for any
J\subseteq[d]
with
|J|=2
the intersection
P\cap\mathbb{R}^{J}
is either the Cartesian product
[a_{1},b_{1}]\times[a_{2},b_{2}]
of some two segments in the coordinate axes in
\mathbb{R}^{J}
with
0
in their relative interior, or it is the convex hull
[a_{1},b_{1}]\vee[a_{2},b_{2}]
of two such segments. In the former case, we say
P\cap\mathbb{R}^{J}
is
axis-aligned
, and in the latter case, we say it is
a diamond
.
Under this condition, define
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
to be the graph on the vertices
[d]
such that
\{i,j\}
is an edge if and only if
P\cap\mathbb{R}^{\{i,j\}}
is axis-aligned. As seen in
[
11
, Lemma 19, Lemma 20]
, if
P
minimizes the number of
faces
then these graphs capture many combinatorial properties of
P
, and specifically they determine whether
P
is a generalized Hanner polytope. We will show an analogous statement for the case where
P
minimizes the number of flags, see
Sections

4
and
4
; these proofs are directly analogous to the ones in
[
11
]
.

Recall that a graph is called a cograph if it is either a single vertex, a complement of a cograph, or a disjoint union of two cographs.
The following two claims will be of use.

Proposition 2.9

(
[
11
, Proposition 12]
)
.

Let
P
and
Q
be proper locally anti-blocking polytopes such that
{\mathcal{G}_{P}},{\mathcal{G}_{Q}}
is defined. Then

1.

{\mathcal{G}_{P^{\circ}}}
is the complement graph of
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
.

2.

{\mathcal{G}_{P\cap\mathbb{R}^{J}}}
is the subgraph of
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
induced by the vertices
J\subseteq[d]
.

3.

{\mathcal{G}_{P\vee Q}}
is the disjoint union
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}\sqcup{\mathcal{G}_{Q}}
, where here
P
and
Q
are assumed to be embedded in orthogonal subspaces.

Lemma 2.10

(
[
3
, Theorem 2]
)
.

A graph is a cograph if and only if it does not contain a path of length 3 as an induced subgraph.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}^{D}}(P)
, sitting ”above”
F
, see
Figure

1
for examples. The precise details are given in the next lemma, which is stated for the case
\dim D=d
for convenience.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
P
be a proper locally anti-blocking polytope. Let
D\in{\Phi_{st}}
be full-dimensional and let
C\in\mathcal{F}_{d-1}(D)
be a facet. Denote by
n
the unique unit normal to
C
such that
D\subseteq C+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n
. Let
F\in{\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1116.82pt{\vbox to1432.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-217.60083pt\lower-1615.20616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}^{C}}(P)
. Then there is a flag
G\in{\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1116.82pt{\vbox to1432.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-217.60083pt\lower-1615.20616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lxSVG@eorulefalse\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-rule} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1845.31 -1741.25 C 1717.64 -1841.62 1568.94 -1891.8 1399.2 -1891.8 C 1318.03 -1891.8 1235.37 -1880.36 1151.24 -1857.49 C 1031.69 -1825.75 971.91 -1780 971.91 -1720.22 L 971.91 -1352.71 C 935.02 -1348.28 908.45 -1346.07 892.21 -1346.07 C 722.48 -1346.07 571.19 -1409.9 438.36 -1537.57 L 438.36 -2233.85 L 329.88 -2233.85 L 329.88 -386.33 C 311.43 -370.1 302.2 -350.91 302.2 -328.77 C 302.2 -278.59 329.14 -253.49 383.01 -253.49 C 438.36 -253.49 466.03 -278.59 466.03 -328.77 C 466.03 -348.69 456.81 -367.88 438.36 -386.33 L 438.36 -544.63 C 578.57 -423.6 728.75 -363.08 888.89 -363.08 C 1116.93 -363.08 1230.94 -461.24 1230.94 -657.54 L 1230.94 -896.64 C 1284.82 -905.5 1337.58 -909.92 1389.24 -909.92 C 1556.76 -909.92 1708.78 -860.85 1845.31 -762.7 L 1845.31 -1741.25 Z M 1791.07 -1716.9 L 1791.07 -867.86 C 1656.76 -932.06 1525.03 -964.17 1395.88 -964.17 C 1333.89 -964.17 1278.91 -959.74 1230.94 -950.88 L 1230.94 -1463.41 C 1230.94 -1523.18 1199.21 -1567.09 1135.75 -1595.14 L 1105.86 -1608.42 C 1043.13 -1636.46 1011.77 -1665.61 1011.77 -1695.87 C 1011.77 -1743.84 1068.22 -1781.47 1181.13 -1808.78 C 1261.57 -1827.97 1336.47 -1837.56 1405.84 -1837.56 C 1547.54 -1837.56 1675.94 -1797.34 1791.07 -1716.9 Z M 1176.7 -1349.39 L 1176.7 -665.29 C 1176.7 -499.98 1080.77 -417.33 888.89 -417.33 C 735.39 -417.33 585.22 -483.37 438.36 -615.47 L 438.36 -1460.09 C 572.67 -1347.18 725.06 -1290.72 895.53 -1290.72 C 995.9 -1290.72 1089.62 -1310.28 1176.7 -1349.39 Z M 1189.99 -1453.44 C 1189.99 -1406.21 1135.38 -1375.22 1026.16 -1360.46 L 1026.16 -1587.39 C 1135.38 -1534.25 1189.99 -1489.6 1189.99 -1453.44 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}^{D}}(P)
such that for any
k\in[0,d-1]
,

(i)

G_{k}\subseteq\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n
,

(ii)

n\not\in\lin G_{k}
, and

(iii)

\supp_{\proj_{\lin C}P}\proj_{\lin C}G_{k}=F_{k}
.

Proof.

For any
k\in[0,d-1]
set
H_{k}=\supp_{P}((\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n)\cap P)\in\mathcal{F}(P)
, and also set
H_{-1}=\emptyset
. Note that for all
k\in[-1,d-1]
,
F_{k}\subseteq H_{k}\subseteq\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}n
, where the latter inclusion is due to the hypothesis that
P
is locally anti-blocking. Therefore
k=\dim F_{k}\leq\dim H_{k}\leq\dim F_{k}+1=k+1
.
By definition we have the inclusion
H_{k}\subseteq H_{k+1}
for all
k
. Note that this inclusion is strict: if for some
k
,
H_{k}=H_{k+1}
then
\dim H_{k}=\dim H_{k+1}=k+1
and so
\aff H_{k}=\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}n
on the one hand. On the other hand,
\aff H_{k+1}=\aff F_{k+1}
, but
\aff F_{k+1}\neq\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}n
since
F_{k+1}\subseteq n^{\perp}
. This implies that there is some
k_{0}\in[0,d-1]
such that for any
k<k_{0}
,
\dim H_{k}=k
and for any
k\geq k_{0}
,
\dim H_{k}=k+1
.

Denote
L=\lin C
. We will build the flag
G
in steps, see
Figure

1
, while maintaining the three
items

(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
from the statement of the Lemma. For any
k\in[-1,d]
, in order, we determine
G_{k}
as follows. Set
G_{-1}=\emptyset
.

If
k<k_{0}
, we have
\dim H_{k}=\dim F_{k}
, so
H_{k}=F_{k}
. In this case
F_{k}\in\mathcal{F}(P)
. Set
G_{k}=F_{k}
.
Items

(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
hold trivially since
F_{k}\subseteq L
, we have
G_{k}\supseteq G_{k-1}
by construction, and
\dim G_{k}=\dim F_{k}=k
(these latter two conditions are necessary for
G
to be a flag). Additionally, since
F_{k}\subseteq C\subseteq D
, we have
\relint G_{k}\cap D\neq\emptyset
.

If
k=k_{0}
, by construction we have
G_{k-1}\subseteq H_{k-1}\subseteq H_{k}
, and also
\dim H_{k}=k+1=\dim G_{k-1}+2
. By the diamond property, there are exactly two faces of
P
of dimension
k
between
G_{k-1}
and
H_{k}
.
Exactly one of these two faces intersects
\relint D
: indeed, if neither intersect
\relint D
then
H_{k}\subseteq L+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}n
, which implies that
H_{k}=F_{k}
and in particular
\dim H_{k}=k
, in contradiction to
k=k_{0}
; and if both faces intersect
\relint D
then
H_{k}\setminus G_{k-1}\subseteq\relint D=L+\mathbb{R}_{>0}n
, which is a contradiction to the fact that
F_{k}\subseteq H_{k}\cap L
. Denote by
G_{k}
the unique
k
-dimensional face of
P
between
G_{k-1}
and
H_{k}
that intersects
\relint D
. Note that
item

(ii)
holds in this case: otherwise,
n\in\lin G_{k}
, and since by construction
n\not\in\lin G_{k-1}
and
G_{k}\supseteq G_{k-1}
, we get
\aff G_{k}=\aff G_{k-1}+\mathbb{R}n
. Since
G_{k}
intersects
L+\mathbb{R}_{>0}n
, we see that
P\cap(\aff F_{k-1}+\mathbb{R}_{>0}n)\neq\emptyset
and so by definition
H_{k-1}\supsetneq F_{k-1}
, in contradiction. Since
G_{k}\cap\relint D\neq\emptyset
and
D
is full-dimensional, we also have
\relint G_{k}\cap\relint D\neq\emptyset
and in particular
\relint G_{k}\cap D\neq\emptyset
.
Items

(i)
and
(iii)
will be verified shortly.

If
k>k_{0}
, again by construction
G_{k-1}\subseteq H_{k-1}\subseteq H_{k}
and
\dim H_{k}=\dim G_{k-1}+2
. By the diamond property there are exactly two
k
-dimensional faces of
P
between
G_{k-1}
and
H_{k}
. Exactly one of these faces
S
has the property
n\not\in\lin S
: indeed, since
n\not\in\lin G_{k-1}
, if both faces have
n
in their linear span then they have equal affine span, which is impossible; however, consider
H_{k-1}
: we have already established
G_{k-1}\subsetneq H_{k-1}\subsetneq H_{k}
, and since
k-1\geq k_{0}
we have
\dim H_{k-1}=k
. Since by definition,
H_{k-1}\subseteq\aff F_{k-1}+\mathbb{R}n
, we see that
\lin H_{k-1}=\lin F_{k-1}+\mathbb{R}n
and specifically
n\in\lin H_{k-1}
. Thus at least one of the faces strictly between
G_{k-1}
and
H_{k}
has
n
in their linear span. Define
G_{k}
to be the unique face strictly between
G_{k-1}
and
H_{k}
with
n\not\in\lin G_{k}
, i.e.
item

(ii)
holds.
Since by construction
G_{k-1}
itself intersects
\relint D=L+\mathbb{R}_{>0}n
and
G_{k-1}\subseteq G_{k}
,
G_{k}
also intersects
\relint D
. By the same argument as above,
\relint G_{k}\cap D\neq\emptyset
. See
Figure

4
.

We turn to verifying
items

(i)
and
(iii)
in the cases
k\geq k_{0}
.
Since we established that
H_{k}\subseteq\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}n
and
G_{k}
is a face of
H_{k}
,
G_{k}\subseteq\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}n
. Assume
G_{k}\not\subseteq\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n
, then
\relint G_{k}\cap(L+\mathbb{R}_{<0}n)\neq\emptyset
, and by construction
G_{k}\cap(L+\mathbb{R}_{>0}n)\neq\emptyset
which also implies
\relint G_{k}\cap(L+\mathbb{R}_{>0}n)\neq\emptyset
. By
Section

2.4
,
N_{P}G_{k}\subseteq L=n^{\perp}
and so
n\in\lin G_{k}
, in contradiction. This proves
item

(i)
.

Item

(iii)
quickly follows: since
G_{k}\subseteq\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n
,
\proj_{L}G_{k}\subseteq\aff F_{k}
, and so
\supp_{\proj_{L}P}\proj_{L}G_{k}\subseteq F_{k}
. Since
n\not\in\lin G_{k}
,
\dim\proj_{L}G_{k}=k
, and so
\dim\supp_{\proj_{L}P}\proj_{L}G_{k}\geq k
. Thus
\supp_{\proj_{L}P}\proj_{L}G_{k}=F_{k}
.

Finally, set
G_{d}=P
. Since
P
is
proper
locally anti-blocking,
\relint P\cap D\neq\emptyset
. We have built a flag
G
that satisfies
items

(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
and such that for all
k\in[0,d]
,
\relint G_{k}\cap D\neq\emptyset
. Since
D
is full-dimensional, this implies
\sign G=D
and we are done.
∎

x
y
F_{0}=H_{0}=G_{0}
F_{1}
G_{1}
H_{1}

A flag
F
(in green) in

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lxSVG@eorulefalse\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-rule} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a proper locally anti-blocking polytope and let
D\in{\Phi_{st}}
be full-dimensional. There are at least
d!
flags of
P
with sign
D
.

This proposition is an immediate corollary of the following lemma:

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the sign
D
. For the minimal sign
\{0\}\in{\Phi_{st}}
there is nothing to prove.

Let
D\in{\Phi_{st}}
and set
n=\dim D
. Assume by induction that for any
C\in\mathcal{F}_{n-1}(D)
,

,

, where

is the unique unit normal to
\lin C
such that

; that
n_{C}^{D}\not\in\lin G_{k}
, and that

.

such that
F_{k}\neq F_{k}^{\prime}
. Since

G_{k}\subseteq(\aff F_{k}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n_{C}^{D})\cap(\aff F_{k}^{\prime}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n_{C}^{D})=\aff(F_{k}\cap F_{k}^{\prime})+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n_{C}^{D}

and
n_{C}^{D}\not\in\lin G_{k}
, we find that
G_{k}
is a subset of
\aff(F_{k}\cap F_{k}^{\prime})+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}n_{C}^{D}
of codimension at least 1. But
\dim\aff(F_{k}\cap F_{k}^{\prime})<k
, which implies that
\dim G_{k}<k
, in contradiction.

We turn to showing
items

(i)
and
(ii)
. For the remainder of this proof, consider
P\cap D
as a full-dimensional polytope in
\lin D
, and specifically, whenever we take the orthogonal complement
v^{\perp}
of a vector
v\in\lin D
, we mean the orthogonal complement in
\lin D
.

\par}^{D}}(P)
. Since for any
k\in[0,n-1]
,

\supp_{\proj_{\lin C}P}\proj_{\lin C}G_{k}=F_{k}\qquad{and}\qquad\supp_{\proj_{\lin C^{\prime}}P}\proj_{\lin C^{\prime}}G_{k}=F_{k}^{\prime},

(3)

specifically for
k=0
, we see that
\supp_{\proj_{\lin C}P}\proj_{\lin C}G_{0}=F_{0}
is a vertex of
\proj_{\lin C}P
, which implies that
N_{\proj_{\lin C}P}F_{0}\cap\lin C
is
(n-1)
-dimensional and thus
E:=(G_{0}+\mathbb{R}n_{C}^{D})\cap P=(F_{0}+\mathbb{R}n_{C}^{D})\cap P
is an edge of
P\cap\lin D
containing
G_{0}
. Similarly,
E^{\prime}:=(G_{0}+\mathbb{R}n_{C^{\prime}}^{D})\cap P
is an edge of
P\cap\lin D
containing
G_{0}
.
Using (
3
) again, we see that
(\supp_{\proj_{E^{\perp}}(P\cap\lin D)}\proj_{E^{\perp}}G_{k})_{k=0}^{n-1}=(F_{k})_{k=0}^{n-1}
is a flag of
\proj_{E^{\perp}}(P\cap\lin D)
, and similarly
(\supp_{\proj_{(E^{\prime})^{\perp}}(P\cap\lin D)}\proj_{(E^{\prime})^{\perp}}G_{k})_{k=0}^{n-1}=(F_{k}^{\prime})_{k=0}^{n-1}
is a flag of
\proj_{(E^{\prime})^{\perp}}(P\cap\lin D)
. Since
E\neq E^{\prime}
, we find two distinct edges of
P\cap\lin D
that satisfy the condition of
Section

2.3
, in contradiction.

Finally, for
item

∎

The inequality part of
Theorem

1.5
is a simple corollary of
Section

3
by summing over all the full-dimensional signs
D\in({\Phi_{st}})_{d}
.

4
Equality case

We follow the strategy of Sanyal and Winter in
[
11
]
. Namely, if
P
is a minimizer for the number of flags, we will show that for any coordinate subspace
H
,
P\cap H
is also a minimizer. This allows us to define the graph
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
of
P
which encodes some of the combinatorial information of
P
, which was recalled in
Section

2.4
. We will see that since all
P\cap H
, and specifically those with
\dim H=4
, will be shown to be minimizers, the graph
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
is a co-graph, and therefore
P
is a Hanner polytope.

.

Proof.

The proof uses
Section

3
to show that if a certain coordinate section does not minimize flags, then any coordinate intersection sitting ”above” it must also not minimize, and specifically the full polytope doesn’t.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}(P\cap H)|=8
. Note that any 2-dimensional
n
-gon has exactly
n
vertices, each contained in exactly 2 edges, and therefore has exactly
2n
flags. Thus
P\cap H
is a quadrilateral. Since it is also locally anti-blocking it must be either
\square^{d}\cap H
or
\lozenge^{d}\cap H
.
∎

By
Section

4
, for any
P
which minimizes the flag number we may define the graph
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
as in
Section

2
.
Our goal now is to present an analogue of
[
11
, Lemma 19]
; that is, to show that if
P
minimizes flags, then it can be recovered from
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
.

Recall the definition of
{\Phi_{st}}
. Any cone
C\in{\Phi_{st}}
can be uniquely presented as the positive span of some subset
J_{C}\subseteq\{\pm e_{i}\}_{i\in[d]}
. Denote by
1_{C}\in C
the vector
1_{C}=\sum_{v\in J_{C}}v
, i.e. for full-dimensional
C\in{\Phi_{st}}
the vectors
1_{C}
are exactly the vertices of the unit cube
\square^{d}
, and for general
C\in{\Phi_{st}}
the vectors
1_{C}
are always barycenters of some face of
\square^{d}
.

We prove the following proposition, which says that the condition
1_{D}\in P
, for
D\in{\Phi_{st}}
, is only deteremined by the condition
\forall C\in\mathcal{F}_{2}(D):1_{C}\in P
.

Proof.

. For

and assume

,

. Pick some

(i.e.

is a standard basis vector, up to sign) and denote

. Note that for any

,

; also

as previously stated. Thus, since

is normalized,

is
(k-1)
-dimensional.

be a flag with

. For any

,

, and so for any

,
F\not\in\Ima\chi_{C}^{D}
by

of

. However,
F_{0}\neq 1_{D}
and
\left\langle F_{0},v\right\rangle=1
(since

), and so
\proj_{C_{0}}F_{0}\neq 1_{C_{0}}
is not a vertex of
P\cap C_{0}
. Therefore
F\not\in\Ima\chi_{C_{0}}^{D}
as well, by
item

(iii)
of
Section

3
. In all, for any
C\in\mathcal{F}_{k-1}(D)
,
F\not\in\Ima\chi_{C}^{D}
, in contradiction to minimality. Thus
1_{D}\in P
.
∎

As a corollary, we see that for normalized locally anti-blocking polytopes
P
with the minimal number of flags,
P
is determined by
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
P
be a normalized locally anti-blocking polytope in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and assume
|\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1116.82pt{\vbox to1432.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-217.60083pt\lower-1615.20616pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1845.31 -1741.25 C 1717.64 -1841.62 1568.94 -1891.8 1399.2 -1891.8 C 1318.03 -1891.8 1235.37 -1880.36 1151.24 -1857.49 C 1031.69 -1825.75 971.91 -1780 971.91 -1720.22 L 971.91 -1352.71 C 935.02 -1348.28 908.45 -1346.07 892.21 -1346.07 C 722.48 -1346.07 571.19 -1409.9 438.36 -1537.57 L 438.36 -2233.85 L 329.88 -2233.85 L 329.88 -386.33 C 311.43 -370.1 302.2 -350.91 302.2 -328.77 C 302.2 -278.59 329.14 -253.49 383.01 -253.49 C 438.36 -253.49 466.03 -278.59 466.03 -328.77 C 466.03 -348.69 456.81 -367.88 438.36 -386.33 L 438.36 -544.63 C 578.57 -423.6 728.75 -363.08 888.89 -363.08 C 1116.93 -363.08 1230.94 -461.24 1230.94 -657.54 L 1230.94 -896.64 C 1284.82 -905.5 1337.58 -909.92 1389.24 -909.92 C 1556.76 -909.92 1708.78 -860.85 1845.31 -762.7 L 1845.31 -1741.25 Z M 1791.07 -1716.9 L 1791.07 -867.86 C 1656.76 -932.06 1525.03 -964.17 1395.88 -964.17 C 1333.89 -964.17 1278.91 -959.74 1230.94 -950.88 L 1230.94 -1463.41 C 1230.94 -1523.18 1199.21 -1567.09 1135.75 -1595.14 L 1105.86 -1608.42 C 1043.13 -1636.46 1011.77 -1665.61 1011.77 -1695.87 C 1011.77 -1743.84 1068.22 -1781.47 1181.13 -1808.78 C 1261.57 -1827.97 1336.47 -1837.56 1405.84 -1837.56 C 1547.54 -1837.56 1675.94 -1797.34 1791.07 -1716.9 Z M 1176.7 -1349.39 L 1176.7 -665.29 C 1176.7 -499.98 1080.77 -417.33 888.89 -417.33 C 735.39 -417.33 585.22 -483.37 438.36 -615.47 L 438.36 -1460.09 C 572.67 -1347.18 725.06 -1290.72 895.53 -1290.72 C 995.9 -1290.72 1089.62 -1310.28 1176.7 -1349.39 Z M 1189.99 -1453.44 C 1189.99 -1406.21 1135.38 -1375.22 1026.16 -1360.46 L 1026.16 -1587.39 C 1135.38 -1534.25 1189.99 -1489.6 1189.99 -1453.44 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}(P)|=2^{d}\cdot d!
. Then

P=\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{G}_{P}}):=\bigvee_{D}1_{D},

where the convex hull runs over all
D\in{\Phi_{st}}
such that
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{J}
for some
J\subseteq[d]
which induces a clique in
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
.

Proof.

Let
D\in{\Phi_{st}}
with
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{J}
for some
J\subseteq[d]
which induces a clique in
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
. By definition of
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
, for any
C\in\mathcal{F}_{2}(D)
,
1_{C}\in P
. By
Section

4
,
1_{D}\in P
. Thus the
\supseteq
inclusion holds.
Let
v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(P)
and set
J\subseteq[d]
to be the set of nonzero coordinates of
v
. If
|J|=1
,
v=\pm e_{i}
for some
i\in[d]
and must be in
\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{G}_{P}})
.
If
|J|\geq 2
, since
P
is locally anti-blocking, for any coordinate 2-plane
H\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{J}
,
\proj_{H}v\in P
is not contained in any of the coordinate axes. Therefore by
Section

4
,
P\cap H=\square^{d}\cap H
is axis-aligned and so the coordinates corresponding to
H
form an edge in
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
. Therefore
J
induces a clique in
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
, meaning
v\in\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{G}_{P}})
and the
\subseteq
inclusion also holds.
∎

Remark
.

As another corollary, we see that any normalized locally anti-blocking polytope that minimizes flags has vertices in
\{\pm 1,0\}^{d}
.

We also need the following direct analogue of
[
11
, Lemma 20]
:

Claim 4.5
.

Let
P
be a normalized locally anti-blocking polytope in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and assume

\par}(P)|=2^{d}\cdot d!
. Then
P
is a Hanner polytope if and only if
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
is a cograph.

Proof.

Consider the map

\mathcal{G}:\{\text{Normalized, flag-minimizing locally anti-blocking polytopes}\}\to\{\text{Graphs}\}

given by
P\mapsto{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
. By
Section

4
,
\mathcal{G}
is 1-to-1. Therefore it is enough to check that
\mathcal{G}
maps the subset of Hanner polytopes onto the subset of cographs. By the recursive definition of Hanner polytopes, it is enough to check 3 cases:

•

If
d=1
,
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
is a single-vertex graph, and so it is a cograph.

•

If
P=Q^{\circ}
for some previously constructed Hanner polytope
Q
, then
{\mathcal{G}_{Q}}
is a cograph by induction, and so
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}
is the complement graph of
{\mathcal{G}_{Q}}
by
Section

2.4
and therefore is also a cograph.

•

If
P=Q_{1}\vee Q_{2}
for some proviously constructed Hanner polytopes
Q_{1},Q_{2}
lying in orthogonal subspaces, then by induction
{\mathcal{G}_{Q_{1}}},{\mathcal{G}_{Q_{2}}}
are cographs. By
Section

2.4
,
{\mathcal{G}_{P}}={\mathcal{G}_{Q_{1}}}\sqcup{\mathcal{G}_{Q_{2}}}
is also a cograph.

By definition, all cographs can be constructed this way from a Hanner polytope.
∎

Appendix Appendix A
Flag number calculation for
\mathcal{C}(\Pi_{3})

By definition,
\mathcal{C}(\Pi_{3})
has vertices

\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1\\
\pm 1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\pm 1\\
\pm 1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
\pm 1\\
\pm 1\end{pmatrix}.

(4)

By
Section

2.4
,
\mathcal{C}(\Pi_{3})^{\circ}
has vertices

\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1\\
0\\
0\\
\pm 1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\pm 1\\
0\\
\pm 1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1\\
0\\
\pm 1\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

For any vertex
v
of (
4
), its vertex figure has the same combinatorial structure as the dual facet of
\mathcal{C}(\Pi_{3})^{\circ}
to
v
, see
[
16
, Section 2]
. Therefore the number of flags containing
v
is the number of flags of the dual facet. For vertices in the first and third columns of (
4
), the dual facet is an affine image of the polytope with vertices

\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1\\
0\\
\pm 1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\pm 1\\
0\end{pmatrix},

see
Figure

5
.

Note that every edge of a 3-dimensional polytope contains exactly 2 vertices and is contained in exactly 2 facets. Therefore there are exactly 4 flags that contain every edge, and so the above polytope has exactly 44 flags. For vertices of (
4
) of the second column, the dual facet is an affine image of the polytope with vertices

\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
\pm 1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1\\
-1\\
0\end{pmatrix},

which is simply a 3-dimensional simplex, and so has 24 flags. In all, there are
8\cdot 44+4\cdot 24=448
flags in
\mathcal{C}(\Pi_{3})
.

Figure 5:
The dual facet for vertices in the first and third columns.
